k < d, is independent of R. Thus d = n2 + 2n − 4 is the smallest uniform reconstruction order
already among these single-point objects.
Proof. Theorem 8.2 applies with B = Spec C and the bundle U . To spell out what its intrinsic
construction uses in this case, the unique maximal ideal n of the finite algebra determines its
tangent space and the multiplication kernel
                                             h                       i
                                    KR = ker Symd (n/n2 ) −→ nd .
This is precisely the degree-d ideal in the chosen presentation; nonlinear changes of generators
contribute only terms above degree d to a degree-d relation. Hence an ungraded algebra isomor-
phism identifies the homogeneous cones generated by these kernels. Their determinant reductions
recover the unordered matrix factors. The residual support ranks (1, M ) rule out transposition,
and the remaining left-right map carries the left coefficient line to the left coefficient line. Exterior
duality then gives R′ = (Sym2 g)R. Conversely such a map, together with any chosen right
identification, induces the algebra isomorphism. There are no relations below degree d, so the
embedding dimension and the lower-order assertion are immediate. The relation subspace at
degree d has dimension M , giving the asserted length. The inequivalent common-factor and
no-common-factor pencils prove uniform minimality as before.                                           □
   In particular the earlier Schubert-line construction is a sufficient geometric realization, not a
lower bound on the dimension of a supporting reduced scheme. The local inverse just proved
retains no curve at all. Its additional ingredient is the coefficient-support asymmetry, which was
not used in the earlier geometric orientation argument. This is a stronger inverse statement, not
a conclusion from knowing the discriminant or the reduced determinant cone.
                                                                                    [d]
8.3. The local automorphism kernel is explicit. Put a = n2 , ℓ = length AR , and GR = {g ∈
GL(V ) : (Sym2 g)R = R}. The symbol n2 in the next statement is the square of the maximal
ideal of the finite algebra.
Proposition 8.4 (Tangent-identity automorphisms). There is a split exact sequence of complex
automorphism groups
                                              [d]
                      1 −→ UR −→ AutC (AR ) −→ (GR × GL(U ))/C∗ −→ 1,                              (8.3)
22                                              QIAN QI


where the scalar subgroup is diagonal. The kernel UR is a unipotent algebraic group whose
underlying variety is an affine space of dimension
                                              a(ℓ − 1 − a).
More explicitly, after choosing the a degree-one generators x1 , . . . , xa , its elements are exactly
the substitutions xi 7→ xi + ui with arbitrary ui ∈ n2 . The affine-space parametrization is not an
assertion that this group law is additive.
Proof. The local inverse and the support-rank argument identify the linear stabilizer of KR with
the indicated left-right quotient; transposition is absent. These linear maps lift homogeneously,
giving the quotient and splitting. An automorphism in the kernel is the identity on n/n2 , and
consequently has exactly the displayed generator images.
   Conversely choose arbitrary ui ∈ n2 . Under this substitution a homogeneous defining relation
of degree d changes only by terms in nd+1 = 0, so all defining relations are respected. The induced
map is the identity on the associated graded algebra, hence is invertible as a finite filtered linear
map and therefore as an algebra map. There are no further conditions on the ui . Reading the
generator images and performing substitutions are polynomial mutually inverse maps between the
kernel and the vector space (n2 )⊕a . Since dim n2 = ℓ − 1 − a, this proves the variety and dimension
assertions. On a basis ordered by degree every such automorphism is upper unitriangular; the
algebraic group is therefore unipotent. Its law includes higher substitution terms, which accounts
for its generally noncommutative structure.                                                         □
   The positive-dimensional family statement remains useful after the local inverse: it recovers
an entire varying subbundle and the actual source bundle up to one global left transformation,
whereas separate fibrewise local isomorphisms need not glue. The geometric automorphism
statements here still do not identify unmarked moduli stacks or all deformations over nonreduced
parameter bases.

          9. The intrinsic polarization and a projective moduli completion
  Put P = Gr(2, Sym2 V ), N = n+1              N              2
                                                     
                                   2 , M = 2 , and D = n + 2n − 4. Fix temporarily an
                                              ∗
auxiliary n-space U and put E = Hom(U, V ) . Write L = OP (1) for the Pluecker line and
Γ = Φ(P ) ⊂ Gr(M, SymD E) for the closed image of Theorem 7.3. The group in this section is
SL(V ), acting by congruence on pencils and by its induced action on E, with U fixed. It is not
the full group GL(E).
Theorem 9.1 (Polarized completion of the effective failure moduli). There is an SL(V )-equivariant
isomorphism
                            Φ∗ OGr(M,SymD E) (1) ≃ L⊗M ⊗ CU ,                                (9.1)
where CU is a constant one-dimensional space on which SL(V ) acts trivially. Consequently the
stable and semistable loci of Γ, for the restricted Pluecker linearization, are respectively the images
of P s and P ss . The quotient defined by the complete restricted section ring is canonically
                                                             
                         fail
                                       H 0 (P, L⊗q )SL(V )  ≃ Γ// SL(V ).
                                  M
                        Mn = Proj                                                                (9.2)
                                   q≥0

It is a normal projective variety. It contains the geometric quotient of the simple-discriminant
pencils as a dense open subvariety, and has dimension n − 3. The complement of that open is the
image of the semistable discriminant-zero locus.
   The intrinsic inverse identifies the effective semistable failure stack with [P ss / PGL(V )] and
gives a moduli morphism to (9.2). On framed charts the failure-algebra family extends over all P ,
remains finite locally free, and commutes with arbitrary base change. These assertions do not
                         FINITE FAILURE SCHEMES AND QUADRATIC PENCILS                                 23


require, and do not assert, the existence of a universal ordinary algebra on the coarse quotient or
properness of the entire quotient stack.

Proof. Let K be the tautological relation bundle pulled back by Φ. The coefficient factorization
(7.8) identifies it with B ⊗ F (U ), where B is the quotient-volume coefficient line. Exterior duality
gives B ≃ L−1 up to a fixed determinant character of GL(V ). That character is trivial on SL(V ).
Taking the dual determinant gives (9.1), with the harmless constant factor (det F (U ))∗ and
the corresponding fixed determinant line of V . A trivialization of the constant factor identifies
the section rings; a different trivialization rescales each graded piece and has no effect on its
projective spectrum. The section ring for LM is the M th Veronese of the section ring for L.
Taking invariants commutes with this operation. The Hilbert–Mumford weights are multiplied
by M > 0, so stability and semistability are unchanged. This also explains why one uses the
complete section ring on Γ, rather than assuming surjectivity of unspecified ambient invariant
restrictions.
   Finite generation and the projective good quotient are the reductive GIT construction of [25,
Chapter 1]. The complete section ring of the normal projective variety P is integrally closed: it
is the intersection of the valuation conditions for rational sections of powers of L at the prime
divisors of P . Its invariant subring is integrally closed as well. Indeed an element of its fraction
field integral over the invariants is integral over the original ring, belongs to that ring by normality,
and is invariant. This proves normality of the projective quotient.
   For completeness, the simple-discriminant open is nonempty and stable. In a frame (Q0 , Q1 ) of
a pencil, take the discriminant of det(sQ0 + tQ1 ). Under a frame change h ∈ GL2 it has weight
(det h)n(n−1) , and it is invariant under SL(V ). Thus it is a nonzero invariant section of Ln(n−1) .
It is nonzero at a diagonal pencil with n distinct roots, so its nonvanishing locus is semistable and
saturated. A pencil there has a nonsingular member. Relative to that member, the other member
is self-adjoint with distinct eigenvalues; orthogonal diagonalization identifies its congruence class
with an unordered configuration of n distinct points of P1 . The latter configurations are stable for
the usual binary-form linearization: the root-multiplicity criterion is strict since 1 < n/2 for n ≥ 3.
Their quotient is geometric and separated. It follows that the congruence orbits are closed in the
saturated nonvanishing locus. Their stabilizers are finite: the induced projective transformation
permutes a set of at least three points, and the kernel consists of diagonal sign changes modulo
scalars. They are therefore stable. This gives a geometric dense open of dimension n − 3. The
complement is the image of the invariant section’s zero locus by the good-quotient property. This
reasoning uses classical pencil classification and GIT, not a new classification of binary forms;
compare [4, 25].
   Finally apply Theorem E.2 and the rigidification results of Section F. The Pluecker polarization
is taken on the recovered pencil, not on an arbitrary first-relation orbit. A sufficiently divisible
power of L, for example Ln , has trivial central character and descends from SL(V ) to PGL(V ).
A change of auxiliary frame changes the constant factor in (9.1), not the resulting projective
quotient. On the framed Grassmannian the extended algebras are exactly those of Corollary 7.5.
Their equivariance yields the stated classifying morphisms. Removing ineffective inertia does not
turn these into a universal ordinary algebra on the coarse space, and no such descent is used. □

Remark 9.2 (What is compactified). The completion is the classical polarized pencil quotient
recovered from the intrinsic failure invariant, with its polarization verified by (9.1). It is not a
new construction of classical GIT. Its role is to supply a proper coarse target for the effective
invariant and a specified class of semistable limiting families. Strictly semistable points represent
closed-orbit representatives, not every orbit in their fibres. In particular Segre data on the stack
need not descend to an invariant separating all points of a coarse-quotient fibre. For n = 3 the
24                                             QIAN QI


quotient is a point and its discriminant boundary can be empty. The full GL(E) common limit
of Theorem 25.1 is a different construction.

         10. Spectral divisor fibres and the recovery of degeneration type
   The preceding completion concerns the native congruence action. To retain degeneration
type before passage to a coarse quotient, we associate a tower of binary divisor incidences to a
regular pencil. This construction is intrinsic after the unmarked inverse and is unchanged by a
simultaneous change of the pencil and source frames.
   Let Λ = P(R) be the pencil line and let TR be the cokernel of the universal symmetric map on
Λ. A regular pencil means that its determinant is not identically zero. Thus TR is a torsion sheaf.
Define Dj (R) by
                               IDj (R) = Fittj (TR ),    0 ≤ j ≤ n,                         (10.1)
where Dn = ∅. These are effective divisors on the smooth curve Λ. Write sj = deg Dj . If sj > 0,
let Gj be its binary equation and put
                            Jj = Gj H 0 (Λ, OΛ (1)) ⊂ H 0 (Λ, OΛ (sj + 1)).
Changing the scalar representative of Gj does not change Jj . Let Fj (R) be the whole scheme
fibre at Jj of the degree-one divisor normalization ν1 , with r = 2 and D = sj + 1. For sj = 0 put
Fj = ∅.
Theorem 10.1 (A fibre-theoretic Segre invariant). There is a natural identification of finite
schemes over Λ
                                          Fj (R) ≃ Dj (R).                                     (10.2)
At a spectral point x, write the positive elementary divisors of TR as λx,1 ≥ · · · ≥ λx,rx > 0, and
extend this sequence by zeroes. Then
                                                                       X
                          (Fj (R))x = Spec C[ϵ]/(ϵbx,j ),     bx,j =         λx,i ,              (10.3)
                                                                       i>j

with the empty-scheme convention when bx,j = 0. Consequently
                                        λx,j+1 = bx,j − bx,j+1 .                                 (10.4)
The tower, with its maps to the same intrinsic spectral line, therefore determines the full Segre
symbol. Conversely, the elementary-divisor partitions together with their spectral support on that
line determine the tower. The tower retains the projective configuration of the support as well as
the Segre symbol. It is an invariant of the unmarked finite failure algebra.
   The construction is compatible with base change on every family stratum where the Fitting
ideals in (10.1) define relative effective Cartier divisors of fixed degrees. On such a stratum Fj is
finite locally free of degree sj . These hypotheses are part of the relative statement; no assertion of
flatness across a jump of sj is intended.
Proof. Over the discrete valuation ring OΛ,x , Smith normal form gives diagonal entries with
exponents λx,rx , . . . , λx,1 and
                               P
                                   n − rx zero exponents. The minors of size n − j have greatest
common divisor of order i>j λx,i . This proves the formula for Dj , including its nonreduced
structure. It is the usual Fitting-ideal description of elementary divisors, used here on the intrinsic
spectral line.
   The residual two-space H 0 (OΛ (1)) is gcd-free. Hence Theorem 14.1 identifies the degree-one
normalization fibre at Jj with the scheme of linear factors of Gj . On an affine chart write a monic
linear factor as z − a. Monic division gives Gj = (z − a)H + Gj (a), so the entire factor scheme is
Gj (a) = 0, not its reduction. These descriptions agree on overlaps of the projective line. They
give (10.2) and the local algebra (10.3). Taking successive differences proves (10.4). The common
                        FINITE FAILURE SCHEMES AND QUADRATIC PENCILS                                25


map to Λ is essential: forgetting it separately in each member of the tower would discard the
alignment of the spectral supports.
   Pencil frame changes act on Λ by projective transformations; source congruence changes give
isomorphic cokernels and preserve all Fitting ideals. The intrinsic inverse of Theorem 8.3 therefore
defines this invariant from the unmarked algebra. The equivalence with Segre data uses the
classical elementary-divisor convention of [4, §5].
   For a family with the stated Cartier and degree conditions, the same monic division argument
works over any base algebra, including one with nilpotents. It represents the divisor’s functor
of points, so it commutes with base change. A relative effective divisor of fixed degree on a
projective-line bundle is finite locally free of that degree. Local choices of Gj differ by units and
glue. Formation of Fitting ideals itself commutes with base change; requiring them to remain flat
Cartier divisors is precisely what permits the additional finite-flat conclusion.                   □
Example 10.2 (Equal discriminant, different fibre towers). At a root of multiplicity four, the
partitions (3, 1) and (2, 2) have the same zeroth fibre C[ϵ]/(ϵ4 ). Their first fibres are respectively
C and C[ϵ]/(ϵ2 ); all higher fibres are empty there. Thus the discriminant alone does not detect
the distinction, whereas the divisor-fibre tower does. Both are realized by regular symmetric
pencils: for a block of size l, take the reversal matrix Hl and the symmetric pair (Hl , Hl Jl (α)),
where Jl (α) is a Jordan block. Direct sums realize every indicated partition. Additional simple
blocks may be appended. The example is a statement on the regular-pencil stack, not a claim
that distinct Segre strata always remain distinct after strictly semistable GIT identification.
Remark 10.3 (The two divisor constructions). The primitive first relation of an actual pencil
has exact common factor (det T )n−1 , by Lemma E.1. Its common-factor degree does not jump
when the pencil changes Segre type. The divisors Dj above instead lie on the recovered spectral
line and define auxiliary binary first-relation subspaces Jj . Thus (10.4) relates pencil data to
intrinsically induced divisor ramification, not to a nonexistent change in the conductor of the
original exact-gcd locus. The identification supplies a moduli consequence of the all-binary-fibre
theorem while respecting this distinction.

Part II. Divisor incidence and homological fibres
                    11. Common-divisor strata and intrinsic families
   The coefficient condition in Theorem B.1 is not imposed in this section. We instead retain
the common divisor of a space of first relations. This geometric condition gives a larger smooth
parameter space, includes relations without either factor symmetry, and has a scheme-theoretic
inverse. The distinction between a stratum and the condition on its geometric points is essential
when the parameter base is nonreduced.
   Write Sj (E) = Symj E, where E is a complex vector space. For r ≥ 2, denote by
                                      Xh,r (E) ⊂ Gr(r, Sh (E))
the locus of subspaces with greatest common divisor equal to 1, up to a scalar. Empty Grassman-
nians are omitted throughout. When h = 0 this notation can also be used for r = 1.
Lemma 11.1 (Universal common divisor). Let D = g + h. The spaces of r degree-D forms
whose exact greatest common divisor has degree g form a locally closed reduced subscheme Zg of
Gr(r, SD (E)). Multiplication induces an isomorphism
                                                ∼
                        P(Sg (E)) × Xh,r (E) −−→ Zg ,         ([f ], J) 7−→ f J.                (11.1)
In particular, the common-divisor line and the residual subbundle of a family with classifying map
in Zg are recovered uniquely and commute with arbitrary complex base change.
26                                               QIAN QI


Proof. For each a, multiplication from P(Sa (E)) × Gr(r, SD−a (E)) has closed image: its domain
is projective. The union for a > g is closed. Remove this union from the image for a = g, and give
the resulting locally closed set its reduced structure. Its points are exactly those in the statement.
The inverse image of this set under multiplication is P(Sg (E)) × Xh,r (E). The same closed-image
argument with D = h shows that Xh,r (E) is open.
   The restricted multiplication map is proper, by base change. It is injective on geometric points
over every algebraically closed extension of C, since a greatest common divisor is unique up to
scalar in a polynomial ring. Its differential is injective as well. Indeed, a tangent vector in its
kernel satisfies
                                        f˙ j + f j̇ ∈ f J (j ∈ J).
Consequently f | f˙ j for every j ∈ J. For each irreducible factor of f , some element of J is not
divisible by that factor. Comparison of its valuation gives f | f˙. Equality of degrees makes f˙ a
scalar multiple of f ; then J˙ = 0 in Hom(J, Sh (E)/J).
   In characteristic zero the geometric-point injectivity and this differential calculation imply
that the map is universally injective and unramified. A proper, universally injective, unramified
morphism is a closed immersion [12, Lemma 41.7.2]. Here its image is all of the reduced target.
The defining ideal of a closed immersion with this support is zero in a reduced scheme. This
proves the isomorphism, including its assertion for nonreduced test schemes. Equivalently, one
can check properness on the base change of the original projective multiplication map; that base
change has the same underlying open preimage. Its source is reduced because it is this open
subscheme of the original smooth product.                                                          □
Remark 11.2. A map from a nonreduced scheme is required to factor through the locally closed
scheme Zg , not merely to have geometric points in its underlying set. These are different
requirements. For example, over C[ϵ]/(ϵ2 ) the span of x2 + ϵy 2 , xy specializes to a pair with
common divisor x, but has no factor x + ϵ(ax + by). Comparison of the y 2 coefficient in the first
form proves this. The stratum in Lemma 11.1 excludes this transverse infinitesimal family. No
use of a fibrewise radical can replace its scheme structure.
   Now let dim V = dim U = n ≥ 3, E = V ∗ ⊗ U , and δ = det T . Denote by G ⊂ GL(E) the
stabilizer of the line Cδ. By Lemma 4.1,
                         G◦ = (GL(V ) × GL(U ))/Gm ,            G/G◦ = Z/2Z.                        (11.2)
The second component exchanges the two tensor factors; the diagonal scalars in the displayed
product act trivially on the matrix space. Fix s ≥ 1 and D = sn + h. Inside Zsn , impose the
condition that the common-divisor line is the s-th power of a determinant form after a linear
change of the n2 variables. Call the resulting locally closed scheme Zs,h,r
                                                                      det .

                   [ Lie(G) −→ Hom(J, Sh (E)/J) ],          X 7−→ (j 7→ Xj mod J).                  (11.4)
No invariance of J under either full factor group is assumed. The equivalence identifies stabilizers,
infinitesimal families, and specializations whose classifying maps remain in this stratum.
Proof. The power map P(Sn (E)) → P(Ssn (E)), [f ] 7→ [f s ], is a closed immersion in characteristic

hence by f˙ ∈ Cf . The same closed-immersion criterion as above applies. The orbit of [δ] is the

smooth locally closed homogeneous space GL(E)/G. The preimage of its power orbit under the

first projection in (11.1) is therefore a locally closed subscheme. It is exactly Zs,h,r
   Pulling back along the G-torsor GL(E) → GL(E)/G trivializes the common-divisor form,

over an fppf cover.
   The space Xh,r (E) is open in a Grassmannian and G is smooth. The quotient is algebraic
and smooth [13, Theorem 97.17.2]; see also [14, Lemma 101.33.7]. For completeness, write
a first-order residual subspace as the graph of ϵϕ : J → Sh (E)/J. An infinitesimal change
1 + ϵX replaces ϕ by ϕ + X|J mod J. This proves the tangent complex. Its kernel records
infinitesimal automorphisms and its cokernel records first-order isomorphism classes. The same
torsor description proves compatibility with specialization. No assertion is made about limits
outside the specified common-divisor stratum.                                                    □

   The next step connects these parameter spaces with ungraded finite algebras. It is useful to
state the family condition intrinsically, since an absolute nilradical is unsuitable over a nonreduced
                                e+D
base. Put e = dim E and ℓ = D − r. Consider finite locally free commutative unital algebras A
of rank ℓ on a complex scheme T0 , subject to the following conditions. The map ϵ(a) = ℓ−1 tr(Ma )
is multiplicative. For N = ker ϵ, one has N D+1 = 0; all quotients of its power filtration are locally
free; E = N /N 2 has rank e; and multiplication gives isomorphisms Symj E ≃ N j /N j+1 for j < D.
In degree D its kernel has rank r. These conditions specify the maximal-lower-Hilbert-function
stratum; they do not assert that arbitrary smoothings of a local algebra remain in it.

Proposition 11.4 (Relative first-relation algebra). Every such algebra is locally isomorphic, over
its base, to
                 A(K) = Sym E/((K) + mD+1 ),           K = ker(SymD E −→ N D ).
Its trace augmentation, E, and K are intrinsic and compatible with arbitrary base change in this
stratum. For two such algebras, taking the linear part gives a locally surjective map of isomorphism
sheaves to the sheaf of isomorphisms of (E, K). Its kernel is the smooth substitution group whose
underlying scheme in a local presentation is Hom(E, N 2 ).
   After quotienting morphisms by this tangent-identity kernel and fppf stackifying, the algebras
                            det have stack [X (E)/G].
with first-relation map in Zs,h,r               h,r

Proof. Locally lift a basis of E to N . Nilpotence and multiplication on the successive quotients
make the induced map from the truncated symmetric algebra surjective. A relation with a
nonzero term of least degree j < D would give a nonzero kernel in degree j of the associated
graded algebra, which is impossible. Its entire kernel therefore lies in degree D and is precisely K.
Changing a lift by an element of N 2 does not alter a product of D generators, since the difference
has degree at least D + 1. This also proves the asserted uniqueness of the first relation.
    In this presentation multiplication by a positive-degree element is strictly filtration-increasing.
Its trace is zero over any base ring. Multiplication by a scalar has trace ℓ times that scalar,
proving that the presentation’s augmentation is the specified intrinsic trace augmentation. All
filtration sequences are locally split sequences of vector bundles, so their kernels and quotients
commute with base change.
    An isomorphism must preserve trace and induces a linear map carrying K to K′ . Conversely
such a map lifts locally to an isomorphism by the homogeneous presentations. All other lifts are
obtained by substituting generators plus arbitrary elements of N ′2 ; the inverse is constructed
degree by degree in a finite filtration. This gives the stated kernel and its smoothness, without
28                                            QIAN QI

Remark 11.5. The original algebra stack is not this quotient stack: it has the additional nonlinear
inertia just computed. The phrase effective first-relation stack will refer to the specified quotient
on morphisms followed by stackification. The construction retains the full automorphism group
before taking that quotient. It does not identify algebras with the same geometric fibres, nor
does it discard nilpotent parameters. In particular, its relation to a stratum of first relations is
stronger than a bijection of isomorphism classes over C.

               12. The common-divisor boundary and its normalization
  The exact-common-divisor stratum has a canonical completion which need not be normal. Its
normalization records a divisor, and its failure to be an isomorphism is controlled by two different
phenomena: choices of that divisor and overlap with the residual common factor. We establish
these assertions for arbitrary spaces of forms. No determinant, Cauchy, or Pluecker equation is
imposed in this section.
  Let E have dimension e ≥ 2, put Sj = Symj E and sj = dim Sj = e+j−1
                                                                              
                                                                           j    , and fix
                             g, h ≥ 1,     D = g + h,        2 ≤ r ≤ sh .
Write G = Gr(r, SD ). A degree-g divisor incidence over a complex scheme T0 consists of a line
subbundle L ⊂ Sg ⊗ OT0 and a rank-r subbundle K ⊂ SD ⊗ OT0 such that
                                 K −→ (SD ⊗ OT0 )/(L · Sh ) = 0.                               (12.1)
Subbundle always means that the quotient is locally free. In particular, the form generating L
is nonzero on every geometric fibre. Condition (12.1) is the vanishing of a bundle map, not a
condition imposed only on closed points.
Proposition 12.1 (Canonical divisor incidence). The incidence functor is represented, with its
full scheme structure, by
                                     Xg = P(Sg ) × Gr(r, Sh ).
Its forgetful morphism is multiplication
                                νg : Xg −→ G ,       ([f ], J) 7−→ f J.                        (12.2)
This morphism is finite. Its scheme-theoretic image Yg is an integral projective scheme. If K is a
geometric point of Yg and FK = gcd(K), the underlying set of νg−1 (K) is
                                     {[f ] : f | FK , deg f = g},                              (12.3)
with residual space J = K/f . The incidence construction and its scheme-theoretic fibres commute
with arbitrary complex base change.
Proof. On P(Sg ), multiplication from the tautological line tensored with Sh to SD has constant
rank sh . Indeed, multiplication by a nonzero polynomial over a field is injective. A nonzero
maximal minor locally splits this map, so its image and cokernel are vector bundles, also after a
nonreduced base change. The closed incidence (12.1) is therefore the relative Grassmannian of
rank-r subbundles of L ⊗ Sh . Tensoring by L−1 identifies it canonically with the stated product.
In more elementary terms, K has a unique inverse image in L ⊗ Sh ; its quotient there is locally
free because the quotient of SD /K onto SD /(L · Sh ) is a surjection of vector bundles. This proves
the functorial assertion, including its scheme structure.
   The source of νg is projective. Unique factorization in the polynomial ring over an algebraically
closed field gives (12.3). A fixed polynomial has only finitely many divisors of a fixed degree,
up to scalar. The morphism is thus proper with finite fibres and is finite [15, Tag 02LS]. Define
                        FINITE FAILURE SCHEMES AND QUADRATIC PENCILS                               29


Yg by the kernel of OG → (νg )∗ OXg , as in [16, Tag 01R5]. Its integrality follows from the
integrality of Xg : on an affine open meeting the image, the coordinate ring of the image injects
into the domain which is the coordinate ring of its finite inverse image. Reducedness is therefore
a consequence of this incidence construction, not an extra choice. Representability of incidence
gives the base-change assertion.                                                                □
Remark 12.2. The condition defining Yg is the existence of a divisor of degree g. In several
variables it is not the condition deg gcd(K) ≥ g: an irreducible polynomial of larger degree need
not have a degree-g factor. Nor do we assert that formation of the scheme-theoretic image, or
normalization itself, commutes with arbitrary nonflat base change. The assertion for arbitrary
bases in Proposition 12.1 concerns the incidence functor and the base change of its representing
morphism.
   We next retain the nilpotents of a fibre. This will distinguish a ramified divisor choice from
several distinct divisor choices.
Lemma 12.3 (Fibre equations and the overlap space). Let K = f J, set H = gcd(J), and put
c = gcd(f, H) and k = deg c. Then the tangent space of the scheme-theoretic fibre at ([f ], J) is
naturally, after the displayed representatives have been chosen,
                        ker(dνg )([f ],J) ≃ Sk /Cc,   dim ker(dνg ) = sk − 1.                  (12.4)
                                                                     P
More explicitly, choose a projective coefficient chart f (u) = f + uα mα , with one coefficient
normalized to its value in f . Let M (f (u)) be the matrix of multiplication Sh → SD . Choose a
set A0 of sh rows for which MA0 (f ) is invertible, and let B0 denote the remaining rows. For a
matrix C whose columns form a basis of K, the full fibre on this chart is defined by
                 CB0 − MB0 (f (u))MA0 (f (u))−1 CA0 = 0,        det MA0 (f (u)) ̸= 0.          (12.5)
In particular its completed local ring is the power-series ring in the sg − 1 variables uα , modulo
the entries in (12.5); no radical is taken.
Proof. For each column of C, its possible quotient by f (u) is forced by the A0 rows to be MA−1
                                                                                              0
                                                                                                CA 0 .
The remaining rows are exactly the condition that this quotient works. These quotients span a
subbundle wherever the equations hold, by the incidence argument above. Thus elimination has
introduced neither additional solutions nor discarded nilpotents, proving the fibre presentation
over arbitrary coefficient rings in the chart.
   A tangent vector is a pair (f˙ mod Cf, J).  ˙ It lies in the fibre precisely when
                                    f˙ j + f j̇ ∈ f J for all j ∈ J.
It is necessary and sufficient that f | f˙ j for every j ∈ J; the class of j̇ modulo J is then forced.
Taking the minimum valuation over a basis of J, for each irreducible polynomial, makes this
equivalent to f | f˙H. Writing f = cf1 , H = cH1 , with gcd(f1 , H1 ) = 1, gives f˙ = f1 w with
w ∈ Sk . The scalar tangent direction f˙ ∈ Cf is exactly w ∈ Cc. This proves (12.4) and its
dimension assertion.                                                                                □
Theorem 12.4 (Normalization and the exact normal locus). The finite map
                                    νg : P(Sg ) × Gr(r, Sh ) −→ Yg
is the normalization of the natural degree-g divisor locus. Its dimension is
                                     dim Yg = sg − 1 + r(sh − r).                              (12.6)
For a geometric point K, the scheme Yg is normal at K if and only if FK has exactly one degree-g
divisor [f ] and
                                      gcd(f, FK /f ) = 1.                                 (12.7)
30                                             QIAN QI


Normality at a point is also equivalent to smoothness there. Thus the two precise obstructions are
more than one divisor choice, or overlap between the selected divisor and the residual common
factor. The latter gives the fibre tangent dimension in (12.4), and (12.5) determines its higher
nilpotents.
Proof. The locus of gcd-free r-planes in Sh is nonempty: a space containing xh1 , xh2 is one example,
and it can be extended to every allowed rank r. It is open, since the loci possessing a positive-
degree divisor are the finitely many closed images of projective multiplication maps. It is therefore
dense in the irreducible Grassmannian. Its product with P(Sg ) maps to the locus where the exact
gcd has degree g.
   This last locus is open in Yg : remove the finitely many closed images for divisor degrees a > g.
Call it Ug . Every fibre over a geometric point of Ug has one point and, by Lemma 12.3 with
H = 1, zero tangent space. A finite local algebra over an algebraically closed field with zero
cotangent space is the field itself, by Nakayama’s lemma. The fibre consequently has length one,
not merely one point.
   Here is the local argument which also settles the scheme structure. At a closed point of Ug ,
write A for the local ring of Yg , and B for its finite inverse-image algebra. The map A → B is
injective, and B/mA B = C, generated by 1. Nakayama’s lemma, applied to the finite module
B/A, gives B = A. The non-isomorphism locus of a finite morphism is closed, so the same
conclusion holds throughout Ug . Hence νg is birational. The source is smooth and integral, thus
normal. On affine opens, a finite birational extension A ⊂ B with B integrally closed is the
integral closure of A in the common fraction field: every element integral over A is integral over
B and hence belongs to B. This proves the normalization assertion; compare [17, Tag 035E]. The
dimension follows from the product description.
   If Yg is normal at K, its normalization is an isomorphism there, so its fibre is one reduced
point. Formula (12.3) gives uniqueness of [f ], and (12.4) gives sk − 1 = 0, equivalent to k = 0
because e ≥ 2. Conversely uniqueness and k = 0 again make the entire fibre a single reduced
point. The same finite local Nakayama argument shows that the normalization is an isomorphism
near K. Its source is normal, so Yg is normal at K. The source is smooth, so every normal point
of the target is smooth; the converse holds for every complex variety. This proves both directions
without replacing a nonreduced fibre by its set of points.                                         □
Corollary 12.5 (Universality of the exact stratum). The open subscheme Ug ⊂ Yg represents
the unmarked relation families in the divisor incidence whose residual space is gcd-free on every
geometric fibre. Over it the divisor line and residual subbundle are unique and commute with
arbitrary base change. Moreover Ug , as a locally closed subscheme of G , is the reduced exact-gcd
stratum of Lemma 11.1.
Proof. The inverse image of Ug is P(Sg ) × Xh,r (E), and the proof above shows that its finite map
to Ug is an isomorphism. Its source is reduced, so Ug has the reduced induced structure on its
locally closed support. The incidence functor supplies the asserted universal property. A family
in the ambient Grassmannian which only has the right geometric gcd need not factor through
Yg . In particular, the transverse dual-number family of Remark 11.2 is not admitted by this
corollary. The corollary is a scheme-theoretic identification, not a claim that fibrewise gcd tests
detect nilpotent equations.                                                                      □
Example 12.6 (A nonreduced fibre at a divisor collision). Take E = ⟨x, y, z⟩, g = 1, h = 2, r = 2,
and K = ⟨x2 y, x2 z⟩. Its gcd is x2 , so the only point of the normalization fibre has divisor x. In the
chart f = x + ay + bz, division by this monic linear form amounts to substitution x = −ay − bz.
The two remainders are
                                      (ay + bz)2 y,     (ay + bz)2 z.
                          FINITE FAILURE SCHEMES AND QUADRATIC PENCILS                                31


Their coefficient ideal is (a2 , ab, b2 ). Thus the full fibre is
                                           Spec C[a, b]/(a, b)2 ,                                 (12.8)
of length three and tangent dimension two. It is not a reduced point, despite uniqueness of the
geometric divisor. The family
                                       Kt = x⟨xy + ty 2 , xz + tz 2 ⟩
has independent generators for every t, exact gcd x when t ̸= 0, and the displayed collision at
t = 0. Consequently this nonreduced fibre occurs on the boundary of the exact stratum, not in
an unrelated example.
Example 12.7 (Several branches and a normal gcd jump). For the same g, h, r, E, let K =
xy⟨x, z⟩. The divisors x and y give two points of the normalization fibre. At both points the
overlap is one, so the fibre consists of two reduced points. The point of Y1 is nonnormal. By
contrast, put q = x2 + yz and K = xq⟨y, z⟩, now with h = 3. The polynomial q is irreducible
and is not divisible by x. There is exactly one linear divisor of gcd K = xq, and its overlap with
the residual gcd q is one. This point of Y1 is normal although its gcd degree is three. These
two examples show why neither gcd degree nor the number of geometric lifts alone is a sufficient
normality criterion.
Corollary 12.8 (An intrinsic boundary for first-relation algebras). On the maximal-lower-
Hilbert-function stratum of Proposition 11.4, the algebras whose intrinsic degree-D kernel has
classifying map in Yg have, after removing only tangent-identity substitutions, stack [Yg / GL(E)].
Its normalization is the finite representable morphism
                                     [Xg / GL(E)] −→ [Yg / GL(E)].                                (12.9)
                                                                                              e+D
All members of the universal relation family give finite locally free algebras of rank         D   − r.
This construction imposes no pencil image equations and permits the divisor and residual common
factor to collide.
Proof. For every subbundle K classified by the Grassmannian, Sym E/((K) + mD+1 ) is, as a
module, the sum of Sj for j < D and SD /K. This proves finite local freeness and the rank. The
first-relation proposition identifies the quotient after the specified nonlinear inertia is removed.
Incidence and multiplication are GL(E)-equivariant. Pulling (12.9) back along the smooth frame
atlas Yg → [Yg / GL(E)] gives precisely νg . Finiteness, representability, and the normalization
identification follow by descent; equivalently normality and integral closure may be checked on
these smooth atlases. This quotient is not claimed to classify smoothings outside the specified
Hilbert stratum.                                                                                  □
                                                                                                    n+1 
  For the pencil first relations one can take e = n2 , g = n(n − 1), h = 3n − 4, and r = ( 22 ) .
Every pencil then lies in the exact-gcd open of this canonical boundary. The boundary theorem
concerns the much larger space of all degree-g divisorial first relations, rather than a closure defined
by recovering a pencil and reimposing its Pluecker equations. It determines its normalization and
normal locus, but does not assert a classification of all its linear orbits or of all finite-flat algebra
deformations.

                        13. Formal images and marked divisor germs
  All rings and schemes in the following sections are over C. Write Sj = Symj E, and let
Yg ⊂ Gr(r, SD ) be the scheme-theoretic image of
                     νg : P(Sg ) × Gr(r, SD−g ) −→ Gr(r, SD ),          (f, J) 7−→ f J,
32                                            QIAN QI


where dim E ≥ 2, g ≥ 1, and 2 ≤ r ≤ dim SD−g . The source is smooth, and νg is its finite
normalization: a fibre records the degree-g divisors of the common divisor, and on the exact-
degree-g locus there is one reduced lift. We shall retain the scheme structures of these fibres and
of the image.
Lemma 13.1 (Completed finite images). Let X → M be a finite morphism of complex finite-type
schemes, with X reduced and M smooth, and let Y be its scheme-theoretic image. Fix a closed
point y ∈ Y , put R = O
                      bM,y , and write Bi = O
                                            bX,x for the points over y. Then
                                                i
                                                \
                                    O
                                    bY,y = R/        ker(R → Bi ).
                                                 i
This completed image is reduced. If the cotangent map from R to Bi is surjective, then R → Bi is
surjective. If there are two such points, writing Ii = ker(R → Bi ) gives
                                 O
                                 bY,y = (R/I1 ) ×
                                                 R/(I1 +I2 ) (R/I2 ).
The formal schemes at the different xi are disjoint. A local Artin point of the finite inverse image
belongs to exactly one of them.
Proof. On an affine neighbourhood the image ring is the image of the ambient ring in a finite
algebra. Exactness of completion on finite modules preserves this kernel. The completed finite
algebra is the product of the Bi : the orthogonal idempotents of its Artin closed fibre lift uniquely
through the powers of the maximal ideal. Reduced finite-type complex rings are excellent and
analytically unramified, so their completions are reduced; alternatively this applies directly to the
reduced image ring. For cotangent surjectivity, lift a basis of the maximal ideal of Bi modulo its
square to R. The resulting lifts generate every successive associated-graded piece. Subtracting
successive homogeneous lifts and taking their convergent sum expresses any element of Bi as the
image of an element of R. The finite map factors through the completed image, so this is also a
closed immersion into that image, not only into M . Finally the elementary exact sequence
                    0 −→ R/(I1 ∩ I2 ) −→ R/I1 ⊕ R/I2 −→ R/(I1 + I2 ) −→ 0
proves the fibre-product assertion. The idempotent decomposition gives the last assertion,
including over nonreduced Artin bases.                                                  □
Lemma 13.2 (Coprime lifts and the split codimension). If homogeneous forms a, b have coprime
reductions over a local Artin base A, then (a) ∩ (b) = (ab) in A[E]. The relevant graded quotients
and Koszul kernels are A-flat. In the split-divisor model, with sj = e+j−1
                                                                              
                                                                           j    , e ≥ 2, h > g ≥ 1,
and r ≥ 2,
                        c = r(sh − sh−g ) − (sg − 1) ≥ (r − 1)(sg − 1) ≥ 1.
Proof. Over the residue field the two forms form a regular sequence. In each degree their Koszul
complex consists of finite free modules and has its prescribed ranks. The acyclic-complex criterion
over an Artin local ring, proved by lifting splittings successively along its maximal ideal, gives
exactness and flatness of the lifted cokernels. Thus b is a nonzerodivisor modulo a, proving
the intersection identity. For the inequality, express sh − sh−g as the sum of g consecutive first
differences of the sequence sj . These differences are nondecreasing for e ≥ 2. Their sum is at
least sg − s0 = sg − 1.                                                                           □
  In particular the two formal marked lifts in a coprime split model are separated before their
images are compared. Their intersection functor is precisely KA = aA bA LA . Division gives
a subbundle: multiplication by a fibrewise nonzero form is locally split, and the quotient of
the ambient bundle by KA is locally free. Combining this Artin-functor identification with
Lemma 13.1 justifies the completed-image fibre product without any assumption that a tangent-
space comparison determines an intersection scheme.

        14. A global factorization theorem for binary normalization fibres
   For this section E has dimension two. Set N0 = r(D + 1 − r) and m = r − 1. Let
                                     Ts = Ys \ Ys+1 ,                1 ≤ s ≤ D + 1 − r,
with the reduced locally closed structure; an inadmissible Yj is empty. Binary forms split into
linear factors, so the Yj are nested. The multiplication map identifies
                                                 Ts ≃ P(Ss ) × XD−s,r ,
where XD−s,r is the open Grassmannian of gcd-free residual spaces. The first projection is the
algebraic common-divisor morphism, not a choice made independently on individual fibres.
   For nonnegative a, b define Ha,b = C when ab = 0. Otherwise, put k = min(a, b) and
l = max(a, b), and set

Proof. Over Ts the common divisor G and residual subbundle L are defined scheme-theoretically
by the inverse to the exact-divisor incidence. Locally on any base change, choose a member of
L coprime to G on each relevant geometric fibre. There is such a member over C, since only
finitely many roots must be avoided. Regular-sequence cancellation, or monic division and its
flat quotient, gives f | GL if and only if f | G. This equivalence is an equality of incidence
functors over nonreduced bases. The residual subspace is then forced. This proves the base-change
description.
   The displayed multiplication map is finite by properness and the finiteness of the divisors of a
fixed binary form. Its source and target are smooth of dimension s. A finite map between these
smooth spaces is flat: a regular system of parameters in the target is a system of parameters in
each source local ring, hence a regular sequence; the local flatness criterion applies. Its degree
can be computed at a squarefree form, where the gs divisors are reduced and distinct.
   Coprime root clusters split uniquely over Artin bases by Hensel factorization. On the cluster
with polynomial xsi , choosing a degree-ai factor is the equation fi Hi = xsi with both factors
monic. Eliminating one factor by reciprocal expansion gives (14.1). This proves the full product
34                                                       QIAN QI


decomposition, not only its closed points. Its equations have their sole common zero at the
origin: a monic factor of xa+b over C is a power of x. Thus its k equations in k variables are a
homogeneous system of parameters and a regular sequence. The Hilbert series is the quotient            of
the corresponding weighted polynomial Hilbert series. Taking its value at one gives a+b
                                                                                              
                                                                                            a   ; its top
weight is ab. A zero-dimensional complete intersection has a one-dimensional socle. Equivalently
qkl , up to a nonzero scalar, represents the rectangular Schur class. The identification with the usual
Grassmannian cohomology presentation is classical; a purely algebraic Schur-basis statement is
[24, Theorem 2.7]. Tensoring     the local factors proves all three formulas. The total length identity
is the coefficient of z g in i (1 + z)si . Finally the exact-gcd stratum is the product with XD−s,r
                             Q

above. In the symmetric power of the projective line, root clusters specialize by coalescence, and
every merging is realized by moving the corresponding roots together. This gives precisely the
stated closure order within fixed gcd degree.                                                           □
Remark 14.2. The finite flat statement is on the exact-gcd stratum with its indicated scheme
structure. It does not assert that normalization or formation of scheme-theoretic images commutes
with an arbitrary nonflat base change of the ambient Grassmannian. It identifies a specific base
change of the already constructed incidence morphism.

       15. Conductors and all branch intersections on the squarefree locus

is the proper image of the incidence K = ℓ2 L. This condition concerns the common divisor, not
every member of the subspace.

The whole collection of branch intersections and conductor strata is invariant under permutation

Proof. Choose F0 ∈ K with a simple zero at each of the s common roots, and complete it
to a frame F0 , . . . , Fm . Hensel factorization gives moving roots x1 , . . . , xs in the completion.
Use as coordinates the s root parameters and the ms evaluations uij = Fj (xi ), with harmless
invertible normalizing factors. These are part of a regular parameter system: the evaluation
map SD /K → Cs is surjective because K vanishes on the common-root scheme, and D ≥ s − 1.
Thus Hom(K, SD /K) allows independent variation of all rs jets. The passage to the displayed
coordinates is triangular with invertible diagonal, since F0 has simple zeros. The other N0 − rs
parameters and the s root parameters give exactly q = N0 − ms smooth parameters.
    A marked degree-g divisor near this fibre chooses a subset A of these roots. Its incidence
equations are exactly uij = 0 for i ∈ A, with no nilpotents suppressed. There  T
                                                                                     are no other divisor
lifts. Lemma 13.1 therefore identifies the completed image ideal with |A|=g IA . A monomial
belongs to this intersection exactly when its block support meets every g-element subset, or
equivalently has at least s − g + 1 = t + 1 blocks. This proves (15.1). Sums of the coordinate
ideals give all intersections, including intersections of three or more branches.
    For completeness compute the conductor inside the product of the branch rings. Its component
on branch A consists of functions    T
                                         whose extension by zero on every
                                                                        Q
                                                                              other branch belongs to
the image ring. The image of B̸=A IB modulo IA is precisely i∈A            /  (ui ). Indeed a surviving
monomial has support outside A, and must meet every g-subset other than A. Replacing one
element of A by each i ∈    / A forces all those blocks to occur. Conversely their product meets every
such subset. Hence a conductor monomial has exactly t surviving blocks; monomials on more
blocks are zero. This is Jt /Jt+1 . The same coordinates identify Yg+1 with Jt , proving the sheaf
equality by faithful flatness of completion. The case t = 0 means that the conductor is the unit
ideal and the next stratum is absent locally.
    The ring is the ring of tuples on its coordinate branches that agree on every pairwise intersection.
To verify this directly, compare the coefficient of each monomial on all branches where it survives;
the pairwise agreements give a unique common coefficient. If an element b of the total fraction
ring has b2 , b3 in the image, it first belongs to the normalization, by integrality. Its two restrictions
on any intersection have equal squares and cubes, hence are equal in that reduced ring. Thus b
lies in the image. This proves seminormality by the square-and-cube criterion.
    Formula (15.3) follows by sorting nonzero         monomials by their block support. The top-
                                               s
dimensional    contributions come from the t supports of size t, giving dimension mt and multiplicity
  s
  t  . We supply   the homological step for the depth, since branch counting alone does not give
it. The simplicial complex of the squarefree monomial ideal Jt+1 consists of sets using at most t
blocks. Any induced subcomplex meeting b blocks is homotopy equivalent to the (t − 1)-skeleton of
the simplex on those b blocks: collapse each block to a representative vertex. The two composites
are contiguous to the identity, because adjoining the representatives never increases the number
of blocks. If b ≤ t the     complex is a simplex; otherwise its only reduced homology is in degree
                     b−1
t − 1, with rank t . This last assertion follows also by the elementary induction that adds the
last vertex to a simplex skeleton.
    The squarefree multidegree-W part of the Koszul complex on all sm variables is the augmented
simplicial cochain complex of this induced subcomplex, with degree shifted by |W | − 1. Non-
squarefree multidegrees are contractible in the relevant summands, by pairing faces using a variable
whose exponent exceeds one. Consequently a nonzero positive Betti number has homological
degree |W | − t. The full vertex set gives the nonzero degree sm − t, since s > t when g > 0. The
projective dimension is therefore sm − t, and the Auslander–Buchsbaum formula gives depth
t. When t = 0 the quotient is simply C. Adjoining q power-series parameters gives depth
q + t in every case, and comparison with q + mt proves the Cohen–Macaulay criterion. All the
constructions commute with relabelling roots, proving descent.                                          □
36                                                QIAN QI


Corollary 15.2 (Binary divisor-image intersections). For admissible binary divisor degrees
a1 , . . . , av , their scheme-theoretic intersection is Ymax ai . On the squarefree locus all multiple
intersections of normalization branches, rather than only the degree images, are given by the
union-of-subsets rule in Theorem 15.1.
Proof. The reduced binary images are nested as closed subschemes: a binary form of degree
b has a divisor of each degree a ≤ b, and reduced closed subschemes are determined by their
closed points. Their ideals are thus nested, so their sum is the largest ideal. The assertion about
branches has already been proved as an identity of coordinate ideals, without taking radicals. □

                   16. An incidence atlas for all binary root collisions
  The squarefree theorem has a uniform replacement when any number of root clusters collide.
This replacement specifies the full completed image, not merely a transverse family in it.

Here T = C[[coeff(Pi ), coeff(Rij ), z]]. Degree-zero monic factors are one, and negative-degree

Proof. Choose F0 ∈ K having exact order si at every common root. The required nonvanishing

                                                         C[xi ]/(xsi i )

                                       SD /K −→
                                                     i
is surjective: its kernel contains K, and the usual interpolation map from SD is surjective. Thus
the Grassmannian tangent space permits independent variation of all rs jets. Division and
preparation change these jets by an invertible triangular matrix, whose diagonal contains the
nonzero residual values of F0 . Their differentials are independent. Formal inverse coordinates
supply the remaining N0 − rs parameters; this integer is nonnegative since r ≤ D − s + 1.
   A marked divisor near a chosen normalization point splits uniquely among the coprime root
clusters. In cluster i its monic representative has degree ai and divides both Pi and all Rij .
Monic division is equivalent, over every local Artin base, to the unique equations Pi = Ai Bi′ and
Rij = Ai Uij . Conversely these equations give the marked divisor and the residual subbundle.
Thus the normalized incidence functor is represented by the displayed regular ring. It is finite over
T : monic factor coefficients are integral over the coefficients of Pi , while the remaining factors
and quotients are uniquely determined by division. Lemma 13.1 now gives the exact completed
image and the intersection of kernels.
   Changing a frame or a coordinate changes only a presentation of the same functor of a
subbundle with a marked divisor. The mutually inverse changes of incidence data therefore induce
                           FINITE FAILURE SCHEMES AND QUADRATIC PENCILS                                           37


isomorphisms of its complete representing rings and of the resulting finite-image diagrams. This
also proves that the calculation describes the entire completed image. A point of that image over
an Artin ring need not lift to its normalization; no such lifting assertion is used or implied. □

               17. The singular Fitting ideal of every double-root model
  The atlas specializes for s = 2, g = 1 to the following ring, after completion of the square and

 Am = R ⊕ It ⊂ B = C[[u1 , . . . , um , t]],         R = C[[u1 , . . . , um , ∆]], I = (u1 , . . . , um ), ∆ = t2 .
Put wi = ui t. The defining equations are ui wj − uj wi and wi wj − ∆ui uj .
Theorem 17.1 (Closed singular-ideal formula). For every m ≥ 1, the intrinsic singular Fitting
ideal is
                              m+1
          Fittm+1 Ωcont
                   Am /C = (I     + ∆I m ) ⊕ I m t = I m−1 (I 2 , (w1 , . . . , wm ), ∆I). (17.1)
The first equality is a decomposition as an R-submodule of B; the right side is an ideal of Am . It
is not merely an ideal of Jacobian minors left unevaluated. For m = 1 it is (u2 , w, ∆u), and its
radical is the conductor (u, w) for all m.
Proof. There are 2m + 1 ambient variables and dimension m + 1, so the Fitting ideal consists of
the m-minors of the Jacobian of the displayed relations. Evaluate this matrix in B using wi = ui t
and ∆ = t2 . A minor not using the ∆ column has u-degree m. It vanishes at t = 0: the only
remaining rows are the Koszul rows in the w columns, of rank at most m − 1. Hence its terms
have a positive power of t, and belong to ∆I m ⊕ I m t. A minor using the ∆ column has u-degree
m + 1, and hence lies in I m+1 ⊕ I m+1 t. This proves the first inclusion.

is an Am -ideal since multiplication by wi = ui t preserves it. This proves equality. The formula
also immediately gives its radical, without discarding the nonreduced Fitting structure in the

Proof. Since g ≥ k, each defining form has no linear term in the coefficient variables. Thus the
presentation is minimal and (18.2) follows by summing dim Symi W . For d = 1, the regular-
sequence argument in the proof of Theorem 14.1 applies to the k reciprocal relations. In particular
none of them belongs to nI. It gives the stated Hilbert series, length, and the classical rectangular
socle class.
   For general W , taking coefficients of Cj gives an equivariant map (Symj W )∗ → I/nI. Its
source is irreducible. This map is nonzero: choose one coordinate x of W , keep only the coefficients
of xi in every Qi , and set every other coefficient to zero. The coefficient of xj maps to the jth
binary reciprocal relation. Were it in nI before specialization, its specialization would be in the
binary nI, contradicting the preceding minimality. Irreducibility now makes the entire coefficient
map injective. The different j have different weights, so their images are independent. They
generate the relation module by the chosen presentation, proving (18.4) and (18.3).
   If d ≥ 2, the sequence d+j−1

shows that the minimal number of relations is strictly greater than the embedding dimension.
A zero-dimensional complete intersection in a minimal regular local presentation has exactly
that many minimal relations as variables; hence these fibres are not complete intersections. This
proves both directions of the classification. Finally when k = 1 the sole form is Cg+1 = (−Q1 )g+1 .
Its coefficients are, with nonzero multinomial factors, every monomial of degree g + 1 in the d
variables. This gives (18.5), including the socle and length.                                      □

  The binary factorization rings in this section are classical. The additional point of the
multivariate argument is that polarization preserves every full irreducible coefficient space in the
minimal relation module. Neither the Grassmannian identification nor a finite calculation in a
few variables is being used as a claim of priority or as a substitute for that argument.

                    19. Homological invariants of the divisor fibres
  We first extract all graded Betti numbers from the squarefree local model and then identify
a determinant-apolar quotient of the multivariate fibres. A separate exact calculation treats
an entire multivariate fibre, rather than only a quotient or its minimal relations. The grading
conventions will be specified in each case.

The ideal Jt+1 has a (t + 1)-linear resolution. The projective dimension of A is sm − t. In the

The same Betti numbers over the completed ambient regular ring apply to the local model after

Proof. The Stanley–Reisner complex consists of sets meeting at most t blocks. If t ≥ 1, its
induced subcomplex on F has, in each nonempty block, a simplex all of whose vertices have
identical external links. Contract that simplex to one of its vertices: the map and the inclusion
are contiguous, because adjoining the chosen vertex to any face does not change its block support.
Applying this one block at a time gives a homotopy equivalence with the (t − 1)-skeleton of
the simplex on b(F ) vertices. If b(F ) ≤ t this simplex is contractible. Otherwise its reduced
homology is concentrated in degree t − 1, with dimension b(Ft)−1 . The latter assertion follows
                                                                      

by attaching its top faces successively, or from the augmented simplicial chain complex and the
binomial identity for its Euler characteristic. Hochster’s multigraded formula now gives (19.1);
see [26, Chapter 5]. If t = 0, every variable is in the ideal and the Koszul formula gives the same
result directly.
   For a support of size b, the coefficient in (19.2) counts choices of i + t vertices meeting each
selected block. Summing proves the formula. All ideal syzygies therefore have the indicated
linear degrees. Taking F to be all sm vertices gives the nonzero last Betti number s−1
                                                                                                
                                                                                              t   in
homological degree sm − t; no larger degree occurs. When m = 1, Auslander–Buchsbaum gives
depth t, equal to dimension, and the last Betti number is the Cohen–Macaulay type. Completion
and adjoining independent regular parameters are flat operations preserving the minimal free
resolution. These are classical squarefree-monomial methods. The assertion here is their full
specialization to the normalization image model, not a claim of priority for Hochster’s formula or
skeleton resolutions.                                                                             □

For k = 2 and g = 2 or 3, the quotient by the coefficients of Q1 alone is already Bd . In particular
the actual ternary-pencil fibre F6,2 (C8 ) has length at least 4862, independently of its embedding
dimension 44 and its 9867 minimal equations.
Proof. Under the stated substitution, the reciprocal series becomes (1 + Q2 z 2 )−1 . Its odd
coefficients vanish and its even coefficients are powers of Q2 . Since g + 1 ≥ 3, imposing Q22 = 0
annihilates all defining coefficients. This proves the surjection, functorially for linear changes of
W . When k = 2 and g = 2 or 3, the first even defining coefficient is Q22 and there are no further

   Choose coordinates x1 , . . . , xd on W and write Q2 = i yii x2i + i<j yij xi xj . Let X = (Xij ) be

symmetric, with independent entries for i ≤ j, and interpret yij as ∂/∂Xij . Then Q2 (x) acts as
directional differentiation in the rank-one symmetric direction xxt . The determinant of X + zxxt

                                   2 + 2y y , y y + y y for distinct i, j, l, and y y + y y + y y

for four distinct indices. They are exactly the quadratic generators in [27, Proposition 3.1 and
Theorem 3.10]. That theorem proves equality with the full apolar ideal, not merely containment.
Its Corollary 2.7 gives (19.4) and the Catalan length. Alternatively, that Hilbert function counts
the independent minors obtained by differentiating det X. Apolarity to one homogeneous form
gives a perfect pairing between complementary degrees and a one-dimensional socle in degree
d. Replacing polynomial degree by twice that degree proves the weight statement. The length
inequality follows from the surjection. For d = 8 the Catalan number is 4862.
   The apolar Hilbert function and its Gorenstein property are classical results of the cited
determinant theory. The new identification in this setting is the functorial quotient of the entire
family of normalization fibres. A quotient’s Gorenstein property is not being attributed to the
original fibre.                                                                                      □

              20. Quadratic sections of reciprocal normalization fibres
   The reciprocal presentation has a distinguished linear coefficient section on which the entire
algebra, rather than only its minimal relations, can be determined. Throughout this section a
42                                               QIAN QI


decomposition E = Ca ⊕ W is fixed. The section is equivariant for GL(W ); it is not asserted to
be independent of an arbitrary change of that decomposition.
  Put d = dim W , g ≥ k ≥ 2, and h = ⌊g/2⌋. In the algebra Fg,k (W ) of (18.1), let l be the ideal
generated by the coefficients of all Qi with i ̸= 2. Write Q = Q2 and
                               Bd,h = Sym((Sym2 W )∗ )/(coeff Qh+1 ).                              (20.1)
Here the ordinary degree of a coefficient of Q is one; its reciprocal weight is two. These two
gradings will always be specified separately. A partition λ ⊆ (hd ) has at most d rows, each of
length at most h.
Theorem 20.1 (The entire quadratic coefficient section). There is an isomorphism of GL(W )-
equivariant graded algebras

Under the coefficient/differential pairing, Bd,h is the apolar algebra of the hth power of the
determinant of a generic symmetric d × d matrix. Its ordinary-degree decomposition is

The Hilbert function is symmetric. A degree-one element is strong Lefschetz precisely when its

                          length Fg,k (W ) ≥ Ld,h ,            mdh ̸= 0 in Fg,k (W ).              (20.6)
The Gorenstein and Lefschetz assertions concern the section Bd,h , not the full fibre Fg,k (W ).
Proof. After setting the indicated coefficients to zero, the reciprocal series is (1 + Qz 2 )−1 . The
first even integer in {g + 1, . . . , g + k} is 2(h + 1); it belongs to this interval because k ≥ 2. Every
odd coefficient vanishes, and the even ones are signed powers of Q. Thus the specialized ideal is
exactly (coeff Qh+1 ): all its higher powers have coefficients in this ideal. This proves (20.2) as an
equality of ideals, not only an equality of radicals.
   For clarity about the pairing, write Q(x) = i yii x2i + i<j yij xi xj and let X have independent
                                                     P           P

symmetric entries Xij , i ≤ j. Send yij to ∂/∂Xij . Then Q(x) acts by differentiation in the
rank-one direction xxt . Since det(X + zxxt )h has degree at most h in z, the coefficients of Q(x)h+1
annihilate (det X)h . The reverse containment is the symmetric-matrix case of [30, Remark 11,
pp. 8–9]: that apolar ideal is generated by the full general-linear orbit of the (h + 1)st power of a
rank-one differentiation direction. Powers of linear forms span their symmetric power, so this
orbit span is precisely the coefficient space just displayed. No assertion that an arbitrary quotient
of an apolar algebra is Gorenstein is used.
   We record explicitly how the classical representation formulas translate here. In [30, Example        9
and Proposition 10], the surviving highest weights are indexed by k1 , . . . , kd ≥ 0 with ki ≤ h,
                                                                                                 P

and their degrees are iki . Set λi = j≥i kj . These are exactly the partitions in (hd ), of size
                         P                    P
                        FINITE FAILURE SCHEMES AND QUADRATIC PENCILS                               43


   iki , and their polynomial representations are S2λ (W ∗ ). This proves (20.3); the ordinary Weyl
P

dimension formula gives (20.4). The same apolar module is the finite-dimensional highest-weight
sp2d -module of weight hωd , with its Levi character shifted by deth ; see [30, Proposition 16 and
Lemma 13]. For the positive roots ei − ej , ei + ej , and 2ei , its Weyl dimension factors are
respectively 1, (2h + 2d − i − j + 2)/(2d − i − j + 2), and (h + d − i + 1)/(d − i + 1). Their product
is (20.5). Apolarity to one homogeneous polynomial of degree dh gives the perfect pairings and
the socle. Equivalently, the complementary partition (h − λd , . . . , h − λ1 ) gives the dual summand
with the indicated determinant twist.
   The strong Lefschetz assertion is the symmetric-matrix case of [30, P    Theorem 4 and Example 5].
There is a direct argument for the asserted nilpotence index. If ℓA = i≤j Aij yij , then ℓpA = 0 in
the apolar algebra exactly when ∂zp det(X + zA)h = 0 as a polynomial in X. Congruence reduces
a rank-r symmetric matrix to diag(1, . . . , 1, 0, . . . , 0). The last determinant power has degree
exactly hr in z, as is seen by taking X diagonal with independent entries. This proves the index.
Finally a surjective local algebra map surjects on every power of its maximal ideal. The section
has nonzero degree dh, proving both assertions in (20.6).                                            □

    21. Spectral Fitting schemes as power-zero loci in one reciprocal section
   The algebra just calculated supplies a single geometric object in which all spectral Fitting
schemes can be read simultaneously. This avoids constructing a separate binary form after each
Fitting ideal has already been recovered.
   Let V have dimension n and R ⊂ Sym2 V be any pencil. Fix h ≥ 1 and put E      e = Ca ⊕ V ∗ .

                                   Kh = a2h+2 E

                                                              2

construction uses a new formal line Ca and is functorial for isomorphisms of V .
   For 0 ≤ p ≤ hn let Jp be the ideal sheaf on Λ locally generated by the coordinates of
                                       ℓp ∈ (Bn,h )p ⊗ OΛ (p),                                 (21.2)

Lemma 21.1 (The local power-ideal formula). Let A(t) be a symmetric matrix over C[[t]] of
rank r over C((t)). Write its finite elementary exponents as 0 ≤ e1 ≤ · · · ≤ er and put ei = +∞

                                                   Pq              
                                               th       e +seq+1

An empty sum is zero, the last term is omitted if s = 0, and t+∞ means the zero ideal.
Proof. A symmetric matrix over this discrete valuation ring is congruent to
                                      diag(te1 , . . . , ter , 0, . . . , 0).
Here is the required elementary reduction. Divide by the least valuation of an entry. A diagonal
unit is a pivot; if only an off-diagonal entry is a unit, the value of the form on a sum of two
basis vectors supplies a unit because 2 is invertible. Split off the resulting rank-one summand by
elimination and continue. Every remaining unit has a square root in C[[t]], so the units can be
removed from the diagonal. The finite exponents are the Smith exponents, as congruence is in
particular equivalence.
   Congruence acts on every graded piece of Bn,h by an invertible matrix over C[[t]]. It therefore
preserves the ideal in question. The apolar embedding

coefficient ideal of [z p ] det(X + zA(t))h .
